In essence, the terms
\widehat{A}_{t-1}(1)
and
\widehat{A}_{t-1}(0)
have a
mutually normalizing
property.
This mutually normalizing property is used to prove several delicate aspects of the central limit theorem, including the following proposition:

Proposition 5.5
.

Under Assumptions
1
-
3
,

\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\big\lparen y_{t}(1)-\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}_{t}(1)\rangle\big\rparen^{2}\Esym\Big[\Big\lvert\frac{1}{p_{t}}-\frac{1}{\widebar{p}_{t}}\Big\rvert\Big]\to 0\kern 5.0pt.

Proving Proposition
5.5
is challenging because the residuals and the difference of the inverse probabilities cannot be analyzed separately, e.g. using Hölder’s inequality.
The issue is that under
Sigmoid-FTRL
, a small proportion of the inverse probabilities
1/p_{t}
could be large; in fact, some inverse probability terms
1/p_{t}
will not concentrate around the inverse stabilized probability
1/\widebar{p}_{t}
.
This means that some of the terms
\Esym[\lvert 1/p_{t}-1/\widebar{p}_{t}\rvert]
can be large.
The saving grace is that when the difference of these probabilities are large, the corresponding residuals must be small, so that the product in the sum is controlled.
The formal proof of Proposition
5.5
relies heavily on the mutually normalizing property described above.
Given its central role in the proof, we believe that the mutual normalizing property might be of independent interest.

5.3
Variance Estimation and Confidence Intervals

In order to construct confidence intervals, we need to first construct an estimator for the oracle variance
T\cdot\textrm{V}^{*}=2(1+\rho)\mathcal{E}(1)\mathcal{E}(0)
.
However, this oracle variance cannot be consistently estimated because the residual correlation
\rho
does not admit a consistent estimator.
The impossibility of consistent variance estimation is a well-known problem in the design-based framework
(see e.g.
8
;
17
)
.
In light of this issue, experimenters tend to opt for conservative (i.e. upwardly biased) estimates of the variance, which in turn yield conservative inferential procedures.
Conservative variance estimates are typically constructed by first identifying an estimable upper bound on the variance, or
variance bound
for short.

The most common variance bound is due to
27
and was originally derived in the setting of a (non-adaptive) completely randomized experiment.
We proceed by adapting his variance bound to the current setting.
Because the correlation is at most
\rho\leq 1
, we have that
T\cdot\textrm{V}^{*}\leq T\cdot\textrm{VB}\triangleq 4\mathcal{E}(1)\mathcal{E}(0)
, which is referred to as the Neyman variance bound.
Unlike the variance itself, the Neyman variance bound is estimable because it depends only on the squared residuals
\mathcal{E}(1)
and
\mathcal{E}(0)
, each of which may be estimated to high precision.
Interestingly, selecting the Neyman allocation
p^{*}
,
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}(1)
, and
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}(0)
which minimizes the variance and then applying Neyman’s bound is equivalent to first applying Neyman’s bound and then selecting the allocation to minimize the bound.
In other words, by constructing an adaptive design which minimizes the variance of the point estimator, we are also minimizing the expected width of the resulting confidence interval.

In order to construct a consistent estimator for the variance bound
VB
, we focus first on constructing consistent estimates of the squared residuals.
For each treatment
k\in\{0,1\}
, the squared residual may be given as

\mathcal{E}^{2}(k)=\argmin_{\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\big\lparen y_{t}(k)-\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}\rangle\big\rparen^{2}=\frac{1}{T}\boldsymbol{y}(k)^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}\boldsymbol{Q}\boldsymbol{y}(k)\kern 5.0pt,

where
Q
is the orthogonal projection matrix
I-\boldsymbol{X}(\boldsymbol{X}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}\boldsymbol{X})^{-1}\boldsymbol{X}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}
,
\boldsymbol{X}
is the
T
-by-
d
matrix whose rows are covariate vectors, and
\boldsymbol{y}(k)
is the vector of potential outcomes
\boldsymbol{y}(k)=\lparen y_{1}(k)\dots y_{T}(k)\rparen
.
To simplify the notation, we introduce
A(k)=\mathcal{E}^{2}(k)
.
In order to estimate the optimal residuals, we use an adaptive IPW estimator applied to the quadratic form,

\displaystyle\widehat{A}(1)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}Q_{t,t}Y_{t}^{2}\frac{\mathbf{1}[Z_{t}=1]}{p_{t}}+\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{s\neq t}Q_{t,s}Y_{t}Y_{s}\frac{\mathbf{1}[Z_{t}=1,Z_{s}=1]}{p_{t}p_{s}}\quad\text{and}\quad

\displaystyle\widehat{A}(0)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}Q_{t,t}Y_{t}^{2}\frac{\mathbf{1}[Z_{t}=0]}{1-p_{t}}+\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{s\neq t}Q_{t,s}Y_{t}Y_{s}\frac{\mathbf{1}[Z_{t}=0,Z_{s}=0]}{(1-p_{t})(1-p_{s})}\kern 5.0pt.

The adaptive inverse probability weighting is chosen so that
\widehat{A}(k)
is an unbiased estimate of
A(k)
.
Even though this estimate
\widehat{A}(k)
is unbiased for the positive value
A(k)
, there is a small chance that it will take negative values.
To account for this possibility, we define the estimated OLS residuals as
\widehat{\mathcal{E}}(k)=\max\lparen\widehat{A}(k),0\rparen^{1/2}
.
However, the following Theorem shows that
\widehat{A}(k)
is a consistent estimator, and so it takes negative values with vanishingly small probability.

Theorem 5.6
.

Under Assumptions
1
-
3
and Condition
1
, for each treatment
k\in\{0,1\}
, we have that the estimated squared OLS residuals satisfy

\Esym[\widehat{A}(k)]=A(k)\quad\text{and}\quad\Varsym\lparen\widehat{A}(k)^{2}\rparen=\mathcal{O}\lparen\{T^{-5/12}R^{2/3}\}^{2}\rparen\kern 5.0pt.

Theorem
5.6
shows that
\widehat{\mathcal{E}}(k)
is consistent at a rate
T^{-5/12}R^{2/3}
, which depends on both the number of samples
T
and the maximum radius of the covariates
R
.
Under Assumption
3
, we have that
R=o\lparen T^{1/4}\rparen
so that the consistency is always at least
\widehat{\mathcal{E}}(k)-\mathcal{E}(k)=o_{p}\lparen T^{-1/4}\rparen
.
On the other hand, if
R=\mathcal{O}\lparen 1\rparen
, then the estimator
\widehat{\mathcal{E}}(k)
converges at an improved rate of
\mathcal{O}_{p}\lparen T^{-5/12}\rparen
.

It’s interesting to note that
Sigmoid-FTRL
uses adaptive experimentation to ensure maximal efficiency of the point estimator, but this seems to come at the cost of slightly worsened rates of consistency of the variance bound estimator.
We find this trade-off to be unproblematic because efficiency of the point estimator is the first order concern for confidence intervals.
In contrast, the estimated variance bound is required only to be consistent, and the precise rate is inconsequential for asymptotic coverage.
It is an interesting question whether this trade-off is necessary, or merely an artifact of our approach.

The proof of Theorem
5.6
proceeds by analyzing the covariance of the individual terms in the quadratic estimator.
This requires specialized case analysis of subject pairs
(s,t)
and
(k,\ell)
.
For example, if the subject pairs are distinct, i.e.
\{s,t\}\cap\{k,\ell\}=\emptyset
, then the corresponding covariance term is zero.
We refer the reader to Section
C.4
of the appendix for the full case analysis.

Given the estimates of the OLS residuals, we construct an estimator of the Neyman variance bound as
T\cdot\widehat{\textrm{VB}}=4\widehat{\mathcal{E}}(1)\widehat{\mathcal{E}}(0)
.
The estimated variance bound inherits the same rates of consistency:

Corollary 5.7
.

Under Assumptions
1
-
3
and Condition
1
,

T\cdot\widehat{\textrm{VB}}-T\cdot\textrm{VB}=\mathcal{O}_{p}\lparen T^{-5/12}R^{2/3}\rparen
.

The estimator of the variance bound, together with the central limit theorem, yields the following Wald-type confidence intervals: for a given
\alpha\in(0,1)
level, the confidence interval
\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}_{\alpha}
is

\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}_{\alpha}=\hat{\tau}\pm\Phi^{-1}(1-\alpha/2)\cdot\sqrt{\widehat{\textrm{VB}}}\kern 5.0pt,

where
\Phi^{-1}:(0,1)\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
is the quantile function of a standard normal.

Corollary 5.8
.

Under Assumptions
1
-
4
and Condition
1
, the Wald-type intervals cover at the nominal level:
\liminf_{T\to\infty}\Pr\lparen\tau\in\widehat{\mathrm{CI}}_{\alpha}\rparen\geq 1-\alpha
.

6
Conclusion

In this paper, we have investigated the problem of Adaptive Neyman Allocation for AIPW estimators in the design-based framework.
We have presented
Sigmoid-FTRL
, an adaptive experimental design which selects treatment probabilities and linear predictors together.
Assuming mild regularity conditions, the Neyman Regret under
Sigmoid-FTRL
converges at a rate of
T^{-1/2}R^{2}
.
This rate is optimal in its
T
dependence as evidenced by our lower bound of
T^{-1/2}
.
A central limit theorem and variance estimator facilitate the construction of asymptotically valid Wald-type confidence intervals.

There are several avenues for future work.
One of the technical questions raised by this paper is whether the dependence of
R^{2}
in the Neyman Regret is necessary.
We conjecture that the answer is yes, but establishing this would require a more refined lower bound construction.
It is also an interesting direction to explore anytime valid confidence sequences in conjunction with Adaptive Neyman Allocation in the design-based setting.
For example, it would be interesting to understand the extent to which minimizing the width of the Wald-type interval (i.e. the central goal of Adaptive Neyman Allocation) and minimizing the width of the anytime valid intervals may be similar or conflicting goals.

Appendix

Appendix A
Preliminary Results

A.1
Properties of AIPW Estimator

Proposition
2.1
.

If
p_{t}\in(0,1)
for all
t\in[T]
a.s. then the adaptive AIPW estimator is unbiased:
\Esym[\hat{\tau}]=\tau
.

Proof.

By the construction method,
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}(1)
and
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}(0)
are measurable with respect to
\mathcal{F}_{t-1}
.
Hence by the law of iterated expectations, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{E}[\hat{\tau}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathrm{E}\left[\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left\{\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}(1)\rangle-\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}(0)\rangle+\frac{\mathbf{1}[Z_{t}=1]}{p_{t}}(Y_{t}-\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}(1)\rangle)-\frac{\mathbf{1}[Z_{t}=0]}{1-p_{t}}(Y_{t}-\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}(0)\rangle)\right\}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathrm{E}\left[\mathrm{E}\left[\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}(1)\rangle-\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}(0)\rangle+\frac{\mathbf{1}[Z_{t}=1]}{p_{t}}\left(Y_{t}-\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}(1)\rangle\right)-\frac{\mathbf{1}[Z_{t}=0]}{1-p_{t}}\left(Y_{t}-\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}(0)\rangle\right)\Big|\mathcal{F}_{t-1}\right]\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathrm{E}\left[\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}(1)\rangle-\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}(0)\rangle+\left(y_{t}(1)-\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}(1)\rangle\right)-\left(y_{t}(0)-\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}(0)\rangle\right)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}(y_{t}(1)-y_{t}(0))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tau\kern 5.0pt.

∎

Proposition
2.2
.

The normalized variance of the AIPW estimator is given as

\displaystyle T\cdot\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}\rparen

\displaystyle=\Esym\Bigg[\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\Bigg\lparen\big\{y_{t}(1)-\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}(1)\rangle\big\}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{1-p_{t}}{p_{t}}}+\big\{y_{t}(0)-\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}(0)\rangle\big\}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{p_{t}}{1-p_{t}}}\Bigg\rparen^{2}\Bigg]\kern 5.0pt.

Proof.

By the proof in Proposition
2.1
, it is easy to see that
\hat{\tau}-\tau
can be written as the sum of a martingale difference sequence, whose variance can be calculated by summing up the variance of each individual term.
Hence by the law of total variance, the variance of
\hat{\tau}
can be calculated by:

\displaystyle T\cdot\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}\rparen

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle T\cdot\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}-\tau\rparen

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle T\cdot\operatorname{Var}\left[\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left\{\left(\frac{\mathbf{1}[Z_{t}=1]}{p_{t}}-1\right)\Big\lparen y_{t}(1)-\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}(1)\rangle\Big\rparen-\left(\frac{\mathbf{1}[Z_{t}=0]}{1-p_{t}}-1\right)\Big\lparen y_{t}(0)-\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}(0)\rangle\Big\rparen\right\}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\operatorname{Var}\left[\left(\frac{\mathbf{1}[Z_{t}=1]}{p_{t}}-1\right)\Big\lparen y_{t}(1)-\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}(1)\rangle\Big\rparen-\left(\frac{\mathbf{1}[Z_{t}=0]}{1-p_{t}}-1\right)\Big\lparen y_{t}(0)-\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}(0)\rangle\Big\rparen\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\operatorname{Var}\left[\mathrm{E}\left[\left(\frac{\mathbf{1}[Z_{t}=1]}{p_{t}}-1\right)\Big\lparen y_{t}(1)-\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}(1)\rangle\Big\rparen-\left(\frac{\mathbf{1}[Z_{t}=0]}{1-p_{t}}-1\right)\Big\lparen y_{t}(0)-\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}(0)\rangle\Big\rparen\Big|\mathcal{F}_{t-1}\right]\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathrm{E}\left[\operatorname{Var}\left[\left(\frac{\mathbf{1}[Z_{t}=1]}{p_{t}}-1\right)\Big\lparen y_{t}(1)-\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}(1)\rangle\Big\rparen-\left(\frac{\mathbf{1}[Z_{t}=0]}{1-p_{t}}-1\right)\Big\lparen y_{t}(0)-\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}(0)\rangle\Big\rparen\Big|\mathcal{F}_{t-1}\right]\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathrm{E}\left[\operatorname{Var}\left[\left(\frac{1}{p_{t}}\Big\lparen y_{t}(1)-\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}(1)\rangle\Big\rparen+\frac{1}{1-p_{t}}\Big\lparen y_{t}(0)-\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}(0)\rangle\Big\rparen\right)\mathbf{1}[Z_{t}=1]\Big|\mathcal{F}_{t-1}\right]\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathrm{E}\left[\left(\frac{1}{p_{t}}\Big\lparen y_{t}(1)-\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}(1)\rangle\Big\rparen+\frac{1}{1-p_{t}}\Big\lparen y_{t}(0)-\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}(0)\rangle\Big\rparen\right)^{2}p_{t}(1-p_{t})\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Esym\Bigg[\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\Bigg\lparen\big\{y_{t}(1)-\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}(1)\rangle\big\}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{1-p_{t}}{p_{t}}}+\big\{y_{t}(0)-\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}(0)\rangle\big\}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{p_{t}}{1-p_{t}}}\Bigg\rparen^{2}\Bigg]\kern 5.0pt.

∎

A.2
Verification of the Sigmoidal Condition

In this section, we verify that two commonly used sigmoid functions:
\phi(u)=\frac{1}{\pi}(\operatorname{arctan}(u)+\pi/2)
and
\phi(u)=\frac{1}{2}\lparen\frac{u}{1+\lvert u\rvert}+1\rparen
satisfy all the required conditions.

Lemma A.1
.

The choice of sigmoid function:
\phi(u)=\frac{1}{\pi}(\operatorname{arctan}(u)+\pi/2)
satisfies Condition
1
with constants
b_{1}=\pi
,
b_{2}=\frac{2^{5/2}\pi}{3}
and
b_{3}=\frac{2}{\pi}
.

Proof.

We verify the requirements in Condition
1
one by one.

(1)

Condition
1
(1) is obvious.

(2)

The derivative of
1/\phi(u)
and
1/(1-\phi(u))
are calculated as:

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{\phi(u)}\right)^{\prime}=-\frac{\pi}{(1+u^{2})\left(\operatorname{arctan}(u)+\frac{1}{2}\pi\right)^{2}}\kern 5.0pt,

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{1-\phi(u)}\right)^{\prime}=\frac{\pi}{(1+u^{2})\left(-\operatorname{arctan}(u)+\frac{1}{2}\pi\right)^{2}}\kern 5.0pt.

The second derivative of
1/\phi(u)
and
1/(1-\phi(u))
are calculated as:

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{\phi(u)}\right)^{{}^{\prime\prime}}=

\displaystyle\frac{2u}{\pi(1+u^{2})^{2}\left(\frac{\operatorname{arctan}(u)}{\pi}+\frac{1}{2}\right)^{2}}+\frac{2}{\pi^{2}(1+u^{2})^{2}\left(\frac{\operatorname{arctan}(u)}{\pi}+\frac{1}{2}\right)^{3}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2}{\pi^{2}(1+u^{2})^{2}\left(\frac{\operatorname{arctan}(u)}{\pi}+\frac{1}{2}\right)^{3}}\left[1+u\left(\operatorname{arctan}(u)+\frac{1}{2}\pi\right)\right]

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{1-\phi(u)}\right)^{{}^{\prime\prime}}=

\displaystyle-\frac{2u}{\pi(1+u^{2})^{2}\left(-\frac{\operatorname{arctan}(u)}{\pi}+\frac{1}{2}\right)^{2}}+\frac{2}{\pi^{2}(1+u^{2})^{2}\left(-\frac{\operatorname{arctan}(u)}{\pi}+\frac{1}{2}\right)^{3}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2}{\pi^{2}(1+u^{2})^{2}\left(-\frac{\operatorname{arctan}(u)}{\pi}+\frac{1}{2}\right)^{3}}\left[1-u\left(-\operatorname{arctan}(u)+\frac{1}{2}\pi\right)\right]\kern 5.0pt.

When
u\geq 0
, it is easy to see that
\left(1/{\phi(u)}\right)^{{}^{\prime\prime}}>0
. When
u<0
, we have

\displaystyle u\left(\operatorname{arctan}(u)+\frac{1}{2}\pi\right)=

\displaystyle-(-u)\left(-\operatorname{arctan}(-u)+\frac{1}{2}\pi\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-(-u)\operatorname{arctan}(-1/u)

\displaystyle>

\displaystyle-(-u)\cdot(-1/u)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-1\kern 5.0pt.

Hence
1/\phi(u)
is convex.
Similarly, we can prove that
1/(1-\phi(u))
is also convex by the symmetry.

(3a)

By L’Hopital’s rule, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{u\rightarrow-\infty}\sqrt{1+u^{2}}\left(\operatorname{arctan}(u)+\frac{1}{2}\pi\right)=

\displaystyle\lim_{u\rightarrow-\infty}\frac{\left(\operatorname{arctan}(u)+\frac{1}{2}\pi\right)}{1/\sqrt{1+u^{2}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{u\rightarrow-\infty}\frac{1/(1+u^{2})}{-u(1+u^{2})^{-3/2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{u\rightarrow-\infty}\frac{(1+u^{2})^{1/2}}{-u}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1\kern 5.0pt.

Hence we have

\displaystyle-\pi\leq\left(\frac{1}{\phi(u)}\right)^{\prime}\leq 0\kern 5.0pt.

(3b)

Let
\theta=\operatorname{arctan}(u)
. Then
1+u^{2}=\operatorname{sec}^{2}(\theta)
. Hence

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{\phi(u)}\right)^{{}^{\prime\prime}}=

\displaystyle\frac{2}{\pi^{2}(1+u^{2})^{2}\left(\frac{\operatorname{arctan}(u)}{\pi}+\frac{1}{2}\right)^{3}}\left[1+u\left(\operatorname{arctan}(u)+\frac{1}{2}\pi\right)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2}{\pi^{2}\operatorname{sec}^{4}(\theta)\left(\frac{\theta}{\pi}+\frac{1}{2}\right)^{3}}\left[1+\operatorname{tan}(\theta)\left(\theta+\frac{1}{2}\pi\right)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2\pi\operatorname{cos}^{3}(\theta)}{\left(\theta+\frac{1}{2}\pi\right)^{3}}\left[\operatorname{cos}(\theta)+\operatorname{sin}(\theta)\left(\theta+\frac{1}{2}\pi\right)\right]\kern 5.0pt.

Since
(1+u^{2})^{3/2}=\operatorname{sec}^{3}(\theta)
, we have

\displaystyle(1+u^{2})^{3/2}\left(\frac{1}{\phi(u)}\right)^{{}^{\prime\prime}}=\frac{2\pi}{\left(\theta+\frac{1}{2}\pi\right)^{3}}\left[\operatorname{cos}(\theta)+\operatorname{sin}(\theta)\left(\theta+\frac{1}{2}\pi\right)\right]\kern 5.0pt.

Let
t=\theta+\frac{1}{2}\pi\in(0,\pi)
and
f(t)=\frac{\sin t-t\cos t}{t^{3}}
. By direct calculation, we have

\displaystyle f^{\prime}(t)=\frac{\sin t(t^{2}-3)+3t\cos t}{t^{4}}\triangleq\frac{k(t)}{t^{4}}\kern 5.0pt.

By direct calculation, we have

\displaystyle k^{\prime}(t)=

\displaystyle\cos t(t^{2}-3)+2t\sin t+3\cos t-3t\sin t=t(t\cos t-\sin t)\kern 5.0pt.

Since when
t=0
,
t\cos t-\sin t=0
and for any
t\in(0,2\pi)
, we have

\displaystyle(t\cos t-\sin t)^{\prime}=\cos t-t\sin t-\cos t=-t\sin t<0\kern 5.0pt.

Hence we have
k^{\prime}(t)<0
for any
t\in(0,\pi)
. Since
k(0)=0
, this implies that
k(t)<0
for any
t\in(0,\pi)
. This further indicates that
f^{\prime}(t)<0
for any
t\in(0,\pi)
. By L’Hopital’s rule, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{t\downarrow 0}f(t)=

\displaystyle\lim_{t\downarrow 0}\frac{\sin t-t\cos t}{t^{3}}=\lim_{t\downarrow 0}\frac{\cos t-\cos t+t\sin t}{3t^{2}}=\frac{1}{3}\kern 5.0pt.

we have proved that for any
t\in(0,2\pi)
,
f(t)\leq 1/3
. This implies that for any
\theta\in(-\pi/2,\pi/2)
,

\displaystyle(1+u^{2})^{3/2}\left(\frac{1}{\phi(u)}\right)^{{}^{\prime\prime}}=\frac{2\pi}{\left(\theta+\frac{1}{2}\pi\right)^{3}}\left[\operatorname{cos}(\theta)+\operatorname{sin}(\theta)\left(\theta+\frac{1}{2}\pi\right)\right]\leq\frac{2\pi}{3}\kern 5.0pt.

Hence for any
u\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{\phi(u)}\right)^{{}^{\prime\prime}}\leq\frac{2\pi}{3}(1+u^{2})^{-3/2}\leq\frac{2\pi}{3}\cdot 2^{3/2}(1+|u|)^{-3}=\frac{2^{5/2}\pi}{3}(1+|u|)^{-3}\kern 5.0pt.

(3c)

By the same argument as in the previous part, since
f(\pi)=1/\pi^{2}
, we have proved that for any
t\in(\pi/2,\pi)
,
f(t)\geq 1/\pi^{2}
. This implies that for any
\theta\in(0,\pi/2)
,

\displaystyle(1+u^{2})^{3/2}\left(\frac{1}{\phi(u)}\right)^{{}^{\prime\prime}}=\frac{2\pi}{\left(\theta+\frac{1}{2}\pi\right)^{3}}\left[\operatorname{cos}(\theta)+\operatorname{sin}(\theta)\left(\theta+\frac{1}{2}\pi\right)\right]\geq\frac{2}{\pi}\kern 5.0pt.

Hence for any
u\geq 0
, we have

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{\phi(u)}\right)^{{}^{\prime\prime}}\geq\frac{2}{\pi}(1+u^{2})^{-3/2}\geq\frac{2}{\pi}(1+u)^{-3}\kern 5.0pt.

∎

Lemma A.2
.

The choice of sigmoid function:
\phi(u)=\frac{1}{2}\lparen\frac{u}{1+\lvert u\rvert}+1\rparen
satisfies Condition
1
with constants
b_{1}=2
,
b_{2}=8
and
b_{3}=1
.

Proof.

We verify the requirements in Condition
1
one by one.

(1)

Condition
1
(1) is obvious.

(2)

The derivative of
1/\phi(u)
and
1/(1-\phi(u))
are calculated as:

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{\phi(u)}\right)^{\prime}=

\displaystyle\begin{cases}-\frac{2}{(2u+1)^{2}}&\text{if $u\geq 0$}\\
-2&\text{if $u\leq 0$}\end{cases}\kern 5.0pt,

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{1-\phi(u)}\right)^{\prime}=

\displaystyle\begin{cases}2&\text{if $u\geq 0$}\\
\frac{2}{(1-2u)^{2}}&\text{if $u\leq 0$}\end{cases}\kern 5.0pt.

The second derivative of
1/\phi(u)
and
1/(1-\phi(u))
are calculated as:

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{\phi(u)}\right)^{{}^{\prime\prime}}=

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\frac{8}{(2u+1)^{3}}&\text{if $u\geq 0$}\\
0&\text{if $u\leq 0$}\end{cases}\kern 5.0pt,

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{1-\phi(u)}\right)^{{}^{\prime\prime}}=

\displaystyle\begin{cases}0&\text{if $u\geq 0$}\\
\frac{8}{(-2u+1)^{3}}&\text{if $u\leq 0$}\end{cases}\kern 5.0pt.

Hence it is easy to see that
1/\phi(u)
and
1/(1-\phi(u))
are convex.

(3a)

By the calculations in part (2), it is easy to see that for any
u\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle-\left(\frac{1}{\phi(u)}\right)^{\prime}\leq 2\kern 5.0pt.

(3b)

By the calculations in part (2), it is easy to see that for any
u\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{\phi(u)}\right)^{{}^{\prime\prime}}\leq\frac{8}{(1+2|u|)^{3}}\leq\frac{8}{(1+|u|)^{3}}\kern 5.0pt.

(3c)

By the calculations in part (2), it is easy to see that for any
u\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{\phi(u)}\right)^{{}^{\prime\prime}}=\frac{8}{(2u+1)^{3}}\geq\frac{1}{(1+u)^{3}}\kern 5.0pt.

∎

Appendix B
Neyman Regret Analysis

In this section, we aim to perform a comprehensive analysis of the Neyman regret.
Section
B.1
derives a lower bound for the Neyman Regret.
The rest sections derive an upper bound for the Neyman regret.
Section
B.2
presents the technical lemmas used in the subsequent proofs.
In Section
B.3
, we prove Lemma
3.3
, which decomposes the Neyman regret into two components: the probability regret and the prediction regret.
Sections
B.4
and
B.5
provide upper bounds for each of these two components in Proposition
4.7
and Proposition
4.11
, respectively.
By combining the results from these sections, we establish the main theorem on Neyman regret (Theorem
4.1
) in Section
B.6
.

B.1
Neyman Regret Lower Bound

In this section, we first prove the lower bound on the Neyman regret stated in Theorem
3.2
.
We then present Corollary
B.1
and Lemma
B.2
, which together show that Assumptions
1
–
3
are all necessary for obtaining a regret upper bound of order
\mathcal{O}\lparen T^{1/2}\rparen
, or at least sub-linear order.

Theorem
3.2

(Lower Bound)
.

For all integers
T
and any adaptive experimental design
\boldsymbol{\Pi}
, there exists a sequence
\{y_{t}(1),y_{t}(0),\boldsymbol{x}_{t}\}_{t=1}^{T}
satisfying Assumptions
1
-
3
and a constant
c>0
, such that the corresponding Neyman regret is at least
\mathcal{R}^{\textrm{Neyman}}_{T}\geq c\cdot T^{-1/2}
.

Proof.

For simplicity, we only prove the case where
d=1
and
R=1
.
Let
\boldsymbol{x}_{t}=1
for any
t\in[T]
.
We generate the random sequence
\{y_{t}(1),y_{t}(0)\}
by the following: Generate
\epsilon_{1},\ldots,\epsilon_{T}
as i.i.d. rademacher random variables. Generate an independent random vector
\boldsymbol{D}=(D_{1},D_{2})
that takes value
(2,4)
with probability
1/2
and takes value
(-4,-2)
with probability
1/2
. Let
y_{1}(1)=D_{1}(T^{1/4}+\epsilon_{1}(1))
,
y_{1}(0)=D_{2}(T^{1/4}+\epsilon_{1}(0))
,
y_{t}(1)=D_{1}(1+\epsilon_{t}(1))
and
y_{t}(0)=D_{2}(1+\epsilon_{t}(1))
for any
t\geq 2
. Define the filtration
\mathcal{F}_{t}=\{y_{s}(0),y_{s}(1):1\leq s\leq t-1\}
. It is easy to show that
y_{1}(1),y_{1}(0)
are independent of
\mathcal{F}_{1}
and
\mathrm{E}[y_{1}(1)]=-T^{1/4}
and
\mathrm{E}[y_{1}(0)]=T^{1/4}
. Hence by AM-GM inequality and law of iterated expectation, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{E}\left[T\cdot\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}\rparen\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathrm{E}\left[\big\{y_{t}(1)-\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}(1)\rangle\big\}^{2}\cdot\frac{1-p_{t}}{p_{t}}+\big\{y_{t}(0)-\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}(0)\rangle\big\}^{2}\cdot\frac{p_{t}}{1-p_{t}}\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{2}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathrm{E}\left[\big\{y_{t}(1)-\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}(1)\rangle\big\}\cdot\big\{y_{t}(0)-\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}(0)\rangle\big\}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\mathrm{E}\left[\big\{y_{1}(1)+T^{1/4}-T^{1/4}-\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{1},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}(1)\rangle\big\}^{2}\cdot\frac{1-p_{1}}{p_{1}}+\big\{y_{1}(0)-T^{1/4}+T^{1/4}-\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{1},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}(0)\rangle\big\}^{2}\cdot\frac{p_{1}}{1-p_{1}}\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{2}{T}\mathrm{E}\left[\big\{y_{1}(1)+T^{1/4}-T^{1/4}-\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{1},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}(1)\rangle\big\}\cdot\big\{y_{1}(0)-T^{1/4}+T^{1/4}-\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{1},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}(0)\rangle\big\}\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=2}^{T}\mathrm{E}\left[\big\{D_{1}\epsilon_{t}+D_{1}-\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}(1)\rangle\big\}^{2}\cdot\frac{1-p_{t}}{p_{t}}+\big\{D_{2}\epsilon_{t}+D_{2}-\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}(0)\rangle\big\}^{2}\cdot\frac{p_{t}}{1-p_{t}}\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{2}{T}\sum_{t=2}^{T}\mathrm{E}\left[\big\{D_{1}\epsilon_{t}+D_{1}-\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}(1)\rangle\big\}\big\{D_{2}\epsilon_{t}+D_{2}-\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}(0)\rangle\big\}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\mathrm{E}\left[(y_{1}(1)+T^{1/4})^{2}\cdot\frac{1-p_{1}}{p_{1}}\right]+\frac{1}{T}\mathrm{E}\left[(T^{1/4}+\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{1},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}(1)\rangle)^{2}\cdot\frac{1-p_{1}}{p_{1}}\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{T}\mathrm{E}\left[(y_{1}(0)-T^{1/4})^{2}\cdot\frac{p_{1}}{1-p_{1}}\right]+\frac{1}{T}\mathrm{E}\left[(-T^{1/4}+\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{1},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}(0)\rangle)^{2}\cdot\frac{p_{1}}{1-p_{1}}\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{2}{T}\mathrm{E}\left[\big\{T^{1/4}+\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{1},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}(1)\rangle\big\}\cdot\big\{-T^{1/4}+\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{1},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}(0)\rangle\big\}\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=2}^{T}\mathrm{E}\left[\mathrm{E}\left[\big\{D_{1}\epsilon_{t}+D_{1}-\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}(1)\rangle\big\}^{2}\cdot\frac{1-p_{t}}{p_{t}}+\big\{D_{2}\epsilon_{t}+D_{2}-\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}(0)\rangle\big\}^{2}\cdot\frac{p_{t}}{1-p_{t}}\Big|\mathcal{F}_{t-1},\boldsymbol{D}\right]\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{2}{T}\sum_{t=2}^{T}\mathrm{E}\left[\mathrm{E}\left[\big\{D_{1}\epsilon_{t}+D_{1}-\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}(1)\rangle\big\}\big\{D_{2}\epsilon_{t}+D_{2}-\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}(0)\rangle\big\}\Big|\mathcal{F}_{t-1},\boldsymbol{D}\right]\right]

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}(18T^{1/2}+1)\mathrm{E}\left[\frac{1-p_{t}}{p_{t}}\right]+\frac{1}{T}(18T^{1/2}+1)\mathrm{E}\left[\frac{p_{t}}{1-p_{t}}\right]+\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=2}^{T}\mathrm{E}\left[D_{1}^{2}\frac{1-p_{t}}{p_{t}}+D_{2}^{2}\frac{p_{t}}{1-p_{t}}+2D_{1}D_{2}\Big|\mathcal{F}_{t-1},\boldsymbol{D}\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=2}^{T}\mathrm{E}\left[\mathrm{E}\left[\big\{y_{t}^{*}(1)-\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}(1)\rangle\big\}^{2}\cdot\frac{1-p_{t}}{p_{t}}+\big\{y_{t}^{*}(0)-\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}(0)\rangle\big\}^{2}\cdot\frac{p_{t}}{1-p_{t}}\Big|\mathcal{F}_{t-1},\boldsymbol{D}\right]\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{2}{T}\sum_{t=2}^{T}\mathrm{E}\left[\mathrm{E}\left[\big\{y_{t}^{*}(1)-\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}(1)\rangle\big\}\big\{y_{t}^{*}(0)-\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}(0)\rangle\big\}\Big|\mathcal{F}_{t-1},\boldsymbol{D}\right]\right]

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{2}{T}(18T^{1/2}+1)+\frac{32(T-1)}{T}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{36T^{1/2}+32T-30}{T}\kern 5.0pt.

On the other hand, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{E}\left[T\cdot\textrm{V}^{*}\right]=

\displaystyle\frac{2}{T}\mathrm{E}\left(\left[\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(y_{t}(1)-\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}(1)\right)^{2}\right]^{1/2}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(y_{t}(0)-\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}(0)\right)^{2}\right]^{1/2}+\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(y_{t}(1)-\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}(1)\right)\left(y_{t}(0)-\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}(0)\right)\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{4}{T}\mathrm{E}\left(\left[\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(y_{t}(1)-\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}(1)\right)^{2}\right]^{1/2}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(y_{t}(0)-\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}(0)\right)^{2}\right]^{1/2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2}{T}\mathrm{E}\left[\left[\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(y_{t}(1)-\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}(1)\right)^{2}\right]^{1/2}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(y_{t}(0)-\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}(0)\right)^{2}\right]^{1/2}\Bigg|\boldsymbol{D}=(2,4)\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{2}{T}\mathrm{E}\left[\left[\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(y_{t}(1)-\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}(1)\right)^{2}\right]^{1/2}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(y_{t}(0)-\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}(0)\right)^{2}\right]^{1/2}\Bigg|\boldsymbol{D}=(-4,-2)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{4}{T}\mathrm{E}\left[\left[\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(y_{t}(1)-\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}(1)\right)^{2}\right]^{1/2}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(y_{t}(0)-\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}(0)\right)^{2}\right]^{1/2}\Bigg|\boldsymbol{D}=(-4,-2)\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{2}{T}\left[\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{E}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(y_{t}(1)-\bar{y}_{t}(1)\right)^{2}\Bigg|\boldsymbol{D}=(-4,-2)\right]+2\mathrm{E}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(y_{t}(0)-\bar{y}_{t}(0)\right)^{2}\Bigg|\boldsymbol{D}=(-4,-2)\right]\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\Big[\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{Var}[y_{t}(1)|\boldsymbol{D}=(-4,-2)]+\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{E}^{2}[y_{t}(1)|\boldsymbol{D}=(-4,-2)]

\displaystyle+2\operatorname{Var}[y_{t}(0)|\boldsymbol{D}=(-4,-2)]+2\mathrm{E}^{2}[y_{t}(0)|\boldsymbol{D}=(-4,-2)]\Big]

\displaystyle-2\Big[\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{Var}[\bar{y}_{t}(1)|\boldsymbol{D}=(-4,-2)]+\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{E}^{2}[\bar{y}_{t}(1)|\boldsymbol{D}=(-4,-2)]

\displaystyle+2\operatorname{Var}[\bar{y}_{t}(0)|\boldsymbol{D}=(-4,-2)]+2\mathrm{E}^{2}[\bar{y}_{t}(0)|\boldsymbol{D}=(-4,-2)]\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2}{T}\left[8T+8T^{1/2}+8(T-1)+8T+8T^{1/2}+8(T-1)\right]

\displaystyle-2\left[\frac{16}{2T}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{4T^{1/4}+4(T-1)}{T}\right)^{2}+\frac{8}{T}+2\left(\frac{2T^{1/4}+2(T-1)}{T}\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{32T+32T^{1/2}+o(T^{1/2})}{T}\kern 5.0pt.

Hence we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{E}\left[T\cdot\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}\rparen-T\cdot\textrm{V}^{*}\right]\geq 4T^{-1/2}+o(T^{-1/2})\kern 5.0pt.

We then prove that there exist a certain configuration of
\{y_{t}(1),y_{t}(0),\boldsymbol{x}_{t}\}
which satisfies Assumption
1
-
3
and the rate of the lower bound is attained.
By construction method, we only need to verify that the squared loss under the OLS is of order
\mathcal{O}\lparen T\rparen
.
By symmetry, it suffices to prove the result in the treatment group
k=1
when
\boldsymbol{D}=(2,4)
.
Since
\boldsymbol{x}_{t}=1
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(y_{t}(1)-\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}(1)\right)^{2}\geq

\displaystyle\sum_{t=2}^{T}\left(y_{t}(1)-\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}(1)\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\min_{\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\mathbb{R}}\sum_{t=2}^{T}\left(y_{t}(1)-\boldsymbol{\beta}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\min_{\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\mathbb{R}}\sum_{t=2}^{T}\left(2+\epsilon_{t}-\boldsymbol{\beta}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\min_{\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\mathbb{R}}\sum_{t=2}^{T}\left(\epsilon_{t}-\boldsymbol{\beta}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\gtrsim

\displaystyle\min\left\{\sum_{t=2}^{T}\mathbf{1}[\epsilon_{t}=1],\sum_{t=2}^{T}\mathbf{1}[\epsilon_{t}=-1]\right\}\kern 5.0pt.

By Hoeffding’s inequality, there exists constant
r_{1},r_{2}>0
such that the probability of

\displaystyle\min\left\{\sum_{t=2}^{T}\mathbf{1}[\epsilon_{t}=1],\sum_{t=2}^{T}\mathbf{1}[\epsilon_{t}=-1]\right\}\leq\frac{T}{4}

is smaller than
r_{1}\exp(-r_{2}T)
.
On the other hand, the regret is lower bounded by
-\mathrm{E}\left[T\cdot\textrm{V}^{*}\right]
, which is of order
\mathcal{O}\lparen T\rparen
. Since
T\cdot r_{1}\exp(-r_{2}T)=o(T^{-1/2})
, there exists a configuration such that
\min\left\{\sum_{t=2}^{T}\mathbf{1}[\epsilon_{t}=1],\sum_{t=2}^{T}\mathbf{1}[\epsilon_{t}=-1]\right\}\geq\frac{T}{4}
.
Moreover, its Neyman regret is positive and of order
\mathcal{O}\lparen T^{-1/2}\rparen
. Hence the result is proved.
∎

The lower bound argument can also be used to demonstrate the necessity of the bounded fourth moment condition in Assumption
1
and the covariate regularity condition in Assumption
2
.
By similar construction as in Theorem
3.2
, we can show the necessity of the bounded fourth moment condition in Assumption
1
.
This is formally stated in the following corollary.

Corollary B.1
.

For all integers
T
and for any adaptive experimental design
\boldsymbol{\Pi}
, there exists a sequence
\{y_{t}(1),y_{t}(0),\boldsymbol{x}_{t}\}_{t=1}^{T}
satisfying Assumptions
1
-
3
except for the bounded fourth moment condition in Assumption 1, and a constant
c>0
, such that the corresponding Neyman regret is at least
\mathcal{R}^{\textrm{Neyman}}_{T}\geq c
.

Proof.

For simplicity, we only prove the case where
d=1
and
R=1
.
The proof is similar to Theorem
3.2
.
Let
\boldsymbol{x}_{t}=1
for any
t\in[T]
.
We generate the random sequence
\{y_{t}(1),y_{t}(0)\}
by the following: Generate
\epsilon_{1},\ldots,\epsilon_{T}
as i.i.d. rademacher random variables. Generate an independent random vector
\boldsymbol{D}=(D_{1},D_{2})
that takes value
(2,4)
with probability
1/2
and takes value
(-4,-2)
with probability
1/2
. Let
y_{1}(1)=D_{1}(T^{1/2}+\epsilon_{1}(1))
,
y_{1}(0)=D_{2}(T^{1/2}+\epsilon_{1}(0))
,
y_{t}(1)=D_{1}(1+\epsilon_{t}(1))
and
y_{t}(0)=D_{2}(1+\epsilon_{t}(1))
for any
t\geq 2
.
This construction differs from that in Theorem
3.2
only in the specification of
y_{1}(1)
and
y_{1}(0)
, where the order
\mathcal{O}\lparen T^{1/4}\rparen
is replaced by
\mathcal{O}\lparen T^{1/2}\rparen
.
By the same proof as in Theorem
3.2
, we can prove that
\mathrm{E}\left[T\cdot\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}\rparen-T\cdot\textrm{V}^{*}\right]\geq 4+o(1)
.
Moreover, one can similarly verify that Assumption
1
is satisfied, except for the bounded fourth-moment condition, for a sequence
\{y_{t}(1),y_{t}(0),\boldsymbol{x}_{t}\}_{t=1}^{T}
such that the Neyman regret is of order
\Omega(1)
.
Hence the result is proved.
∎

The following lemma states that the lower bound of the Neyman regret can also attain order
\Omega(1)
if the covariate regularity condition in Assumption
2
is not placed.

Lemma B.2
.

For all integers
T
and for any adaptive experimental design
\boldsymbol{\Pi}
, there exists a sequence
\{y_{t}(1),y_{t}(0),\boldsymbol{x}_{t}\}_{t=1}^{T}
satisfying Assumptions
1
and
3
, and a constant
c>0
, such that the corresponding Neyman regret is at least
\mathcal{R}^{\textrm{Neyman}}_{T}\geq c
.

Proof.

For simplicity, we assume that
c_{0}=1
,
c_{1}=2
and
R\geq 1
(
R
is shown to be at least order
\Theta(1)
in Lemma
B.5
).
Suppose
\boldsymbol{x}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, where
d=T/2
.
We generate the random sequence
\{y_{t}(1),y_{t}(0),\boldsymbol{x}_{t}\}
by the following:
\boldsymbol{x}_{t}=\boldsymbol{e}_{t}
for
t=1,\ldots,T/2
and
\boldsymbol{x}_{T/2+1}=\ldots=\boldsymbol{x}_{T}=\boldsymbol{0}
.

y_{1}(1),\ldots,y_{T}(1)
are independently sampled from the uniform distribution on
\{\pm 1\}
.
Let
y_{t}(0)=y_{t}(1)
for
t=1,\ldots,T
. It is easy to see that the
y
’s have their marginal expectations as 0.
Now we prove that

\displaystyle\mathrm{E}\left[T\cdot\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}\rparen-T\cdot\textrm{V}^{*}\right]=\Omega(1).

Note that in such construction, the natural filtration
\mathcal{F}_{t}
is generated by
\{y_{s}(1),y_{s}(0),Z_{s}:1\leq s\leq t-1\}
.
By Proposition
2.2
and law of iterated expectation, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{E}\left[T\cdot\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}\rparen\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathrm{E}\left[\big\{y_{t}(1)-\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}(1)\rangle\big\}^{2}\cdot\frac{1-p_{t}}{p_{t}}+\big\{y_{t}(0)-\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}(0)\rangle\big\}^{2}\cdot\frac{p_{t}}{1-p_{t}}\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{2}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathrm{E}\left[\big\{y_{t}(1)-\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}(1)\rangle\big\}\big\{y_{t}(0)-\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}(0)\rangle\big\}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathrm{E}\left[y_{t}^{2}(1)\cdot\frac{1-p_{t}}{p_{t}}+y_{t}^{2}(0)\cdot\frac{p_{t}}{1-p_{t}}\right]+\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathrm{E}\left[y_{t}(1)\cdot\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}(1)\rangle\cdot\frac{1-p_{t}}{p_{t}}\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathrm{E}\left[y_{t}(0)\cdot\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}(0)\rangle\cdot\frac{p_{t}}{1-p_{t}}\right]+\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathrm{E}\left[\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}(1)\rangle^{2}\cdot\frac{1-p_{t}}{p_{t}}+\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}(0)\rangle^{2}\cdot\frac{p_{t}}{1-p_{t}}\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{2}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathrm{E}\left[y_{t}(1)y_{t}(0)\right]-\frac{2}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathrm{E}\left[y_{t}(1)\cdot\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}(0)\rangle\right]-\frac{2}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathrm{E}\left[y_{t}(0)\cdot\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}(1)\rangle\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{2}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathrm{E}\left[\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}(1)\rangle\cdot\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}(0)\rangle\right]

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathrm{E}\left[\frac{1-p_{t}}{p_{t}}+\frac{p_{t}}{1-p_{t}}\right]+\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathrm{E}\left[\mathrm{E}\left[y_{t}(1)\cdot\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}(1)\rangle\cdot\frac{1-p_{t}}{p_{t}}\Big|\mathcal{F}_{t-1}\right]\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathrm{E}\left[\mathrm{E}\left[y_{t}(0)\cdot\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}(0)\rangle\cdot\frac{p_{t}}{1-p_{t}}\Big|\mathcal{F}_{t-1}\right]\right]+\frac{2}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}1-\frac{2}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathrm{E}\left[y_{t}(0)\cdot\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}(1)\rangle+y_{t}(1)\cdot\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}(0)\rangle\Big|\mathcal{F}_{t-1}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathrm{E}\left[\frac{1-p_{t}}{p_{t}}+\frac{p_{t}}{1-p_{t}}\right]+\frac{2}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathrm{E}\left[y_{t}(1)y_{t}(0)\right]

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle 4\kern 5.0pt.

Denote
\boldsymbol{Y}_{T}=(y_{1}(1),\ldots,y_{T}(1))=(y_{1}(0),\ldots,y_{T}(0))
. It is easy to verify that matrix
\boldsymbol{X}_{T}^{{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}}\boldsymbol{X}_{T}
is invertible.
Hence we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{E}\left[T\cdot\textrm{V}^{*}\right]=

\displaystyle\frac{2}{T}\mathrm{E}\left(\left\|\boldsymbol{Y}_{T}-\boldsymbol{X}_{T}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}(1)\right\|_{2}\left\|\boldsymbol{Y}_{T}-\boldsymbol{X}_{T}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}(0)\right\|_{2}+\langle\boldsymbol{Y}_{T}-\boldsymbol{X}_{T}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}(1),\boldsymbol{Y}_{T}-\boldsymbol{X}_{T}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}(0)\rangle\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{4}{T}\mathrm{E}\left(\left\|\boldsymbol{Y}_{T}-\boldsymbol{X}_{T}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}(1)\right\|_{2}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4\mathrm{E}\left(\boldsymbol{Y}_{T}^{{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}}\left(\boldsymbol{I}_{T}-\boldsymbol{X}_{T}(\boldsymbol{X}_{T}^{{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}}\boldsymbol{X}_{T})^{-1}\boldsymbol{X}_{T}^{{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}}\right)\boldsymbol{Y}_{N}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{4}{T}\mathrm{E}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{T}y_{t}^{2}(1)\right]-\frac{4}{T}\mathrm{E}_{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y}}\left[\boldsymbol{Y}_{T}^{{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}}\boldsymbol{X}_{T}(\boldsymbol{X}_{T}^{{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}}\boldsymbol{X}_{T})^{-1}\boldsymbol{X}_{T}^{{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}}\boldsymbol{Y}_{T}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4-\frac{4}{T}\mathrm{E}\left[\operatorname{tr}\left(\boldsymbol{Y}_{T}^{{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}}\boldsymbol{X}_{T}(\boldsymbol{X}_{T}^{{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}}\boldsymbol{X}_{T})^{-1}\boldsymbol{X}_{T}^{{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}}\boldsymbol{Y}_{T}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4-\frac{4}{T}\operatorname{tr}\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{T}(\boldsymbol{X}_{T}^{{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}}\boldsymbol{X}_{T})^{-1}\boldsymbol{X}_{T}^{{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}}\mathrm{E}_{\boldsymbol{y}}\left[\boldsymbol{Y}_{T}\boldsymbol{Y}_{T}^{{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}}\right]\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4-\frac{4}{T}\operatorname{tr}\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{T}(\boldsymbol{X}_{T}^{{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}}\boldsymbol{X}_{T})^{-1}\boldsymbol{X}_{T}^{{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}}\cdot\boldsymbol{I}_{T}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4-4T^{-1}\operatorname{tr}\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{T}^{{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}}\boldsymbol{X}_{T}(\boldsymbol{X}_{T}^{{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}}\boldsymbol{X}_{T})^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4-4T^{-1}\cdot T/2

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\kern 5.0pt.

Hence we have proved that

\displaystyle\mathrm{E}\left[T\cdot\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}\rparen-T\cdot\textrm{V}^{*}\right]\geq 2\kern 5.0pt.

It is easy to verify that Assumption
1
and
3
are satisfied for any sequence
\{y_{t}(1),y_{t}(0),\boldsymbol{x}_{t}\}
generated by this mechanism.
Hence there exists
c>0
and one specific choice of
\{y_{t}(1),y_{t}(0),\boldsymbol{x}_{t}\}
such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{\textrm{Neyman}}_{T}\geq c.

∎

B.2
Technical Lemmas

In this section, we present several technical lemmas that will be used to establish the main results in the subsequent sections.

Lemma
B.3
provides upper bounds for the spectral norms of the matrices involved in bounding the regression coefficients.

Lemma B.3
.

Under Assumption
2
, for any
t\in[T]
, there holds:

\displaystyle\left\|\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}^{{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}+\eta_{t}^{-1}\boldsymbol{I}_{d}\right)^{-1}\right\|_{2}\leq(\gamma_{0}\vee c_{2}\vee 1)((t-1)\vee\eta_{t}^{-1})^{-1}\kern 5.0pt,

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}^{{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}+\eta_{t}^{-1}\boldsymbol{I}_{d}\right)^{-2}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}^{{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}}\right\|_{2}\leq\left(\frac{\gamma_{0}}{4}\vee c_{2}\right)(t-1)^{-1}\kern 5.0pt.

Proof.

For
t\leq\gamma_{0}\cdot\eta_{t}^{-1}
, there holds
\left\|\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}^{{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}+\eta_{t}^{-1}\boldsymbol{I}_{d}\right)^{-1}\right\|_{2}\leq\eta_{t}
. For
t\geq\gamma_{0}\cdot\eta_{t}^{-1}+1\geq\gamma_{0}\cdot T^{1/2}+1
, by Assumption
2
we have
\left\|\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}^{{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}+\eta_{t}^{-1}\boldsymbol{I}_{d}\right)^{-1}\right\|_{2}\leq c_{2}(t-1)^{-1}
, hence for any
t\in[T]
, we have
\left\|\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}^{{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}+\eta_{t}^{-1}\boldsymbol{I}_{d}\right)^{-1}\right\|_{2}\leq(\gamma_{0}\vee c_{2}\vee 1)((t-1)\vee\eta_{t}^{-1})^{-1}
. Suppose the
d
eigenvalues of matrix
\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}^{{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}
are as
\lambda_{1}\geq\ldots\geq\lambda_{d}
. Then by Assumption
2
, for any
t\in[T]
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}^{{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}+\eta_{t}^{-1}\boldsymbol{I}_{d}\right)^{-2}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}^{{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}}\right\|_{2}=

\displaystyle\left\|\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}^{{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}+\eta_{t}^{-1}\boldsymbol{I}_{d}\right)^{-2}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}^{{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\max_{k=1,\ldots,d}\frac{\lambda_{k}}{\left(\lambda_{k}+\eta_{t}^{-1}\right)^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\frac{1}{4}\eta_{t}&\text{ if }t-1<\gamma_{0}\cdot\eta_{t}^{-1}\\
c_{2}(t-1)^{-1}&\text{ if }t-1\geq\gamma_{0}\cdot\eta_{t}^{-1}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\gamma_{0}}{4}\vee c_{2}\right)(t-1)^{-1}\kern 5.0pt.

∎

Based on Lemma
B.3
, we provide the following upper bound on
\Pi_{t,s}
.

Corollary B.4
.

Under Assumption
2
, for any
1\leq s\leq t\leq T
, there holds
|\Pi_{t,s}|\leq(\gamma_{0}\vee c_{2}\vee 1)R_{t}R_{s}((t-1)\vee\eta_{t}^{-1})^{-1}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
B.3
and the definition of
R_{t}
, we have

\displaystyle|\Pi_{t,s}|=

\displaystyle\left|\boldsymbol{x}_{t}^{{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}}\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}^{{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}+\eta_{t}^{-1}\boldsymbol{I}_{d}\right)^{-1}\boldsymbol{x}_{s}\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{x}_{t}\|_{2}\|\boldsymbol{x}_{s}\|_{2}\left\|\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}^{{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}+\eta_{t}^{-1}\boldsymbol{I}_{d}\right)^{-1}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(\gamma_{0}\vee c_{2}\vee 1)R_{t}R_{s}((t-1)\vee\eta_{t}^{-1})^{-1}\kern 5.0pt.

∎

Our proposed algorithm does not guanrantee that
R_{T}=R
.
The following lemma shows that
R_{T}=\mathcal{O}\lparen R\rparen
, which allows us to use
R_{T}
as a proxy of
R
in several established upper bounds.

Lemma B.5
.

Under Assumption
2
, the radius satisfies:
R_{T}\leq\max^{1/2}\{c_{1},1\}R
.

Proof.

Since we have

\displaystyle\lambda_{\min}(\boldsymbol{X}_{T}^{{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}}\boldsymbol{X}_{T})\leq\operatorname{tr}(\boldsymbol{X}_{T}^{{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}}\boldsymbol{X}_{T})=\operatorname{tr}\left(\sum_{t=1}^{T}\boldsymbol{x}_{t}\boldsymbol{x}_{t}^{{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}}\right)=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\operatorname{tr}\left(\boldsymbol{x}_{t}\boldsymbol{x}_{t}^{{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}}\right)=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\|\boldsymbol{x}_{t}\|_{2}^{2}\leq TR^{2}\kern 5.0pt,

which implies that
R\geq c_{2}^{-1/2}
by Assumption
2
.
If
R\geq 1
, then
R_{T}
is guaranteed to be bounded by
R
.
If
c_{2}^{-1/2}\leq R\leq 1
, then
R_{T}=1
.
Hence we have
R_{T}\leq\max^{1/2}\{c_{1},1\}R
.
∎

Lemma
4.12
.

For each iteration
t\in[T]
and treatment
k\in\{0,1\}
,
the adaptive linear predictors are conditionally unbiased for the full-information predictors:
\Esym[\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}(k)\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]=\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}_{t}(k)
a.s.

Proof.

For simplicity, we only prove the result for
k=1
.
By direct calculation, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{E}\left[\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}(1)|\mathcal{F}_{t-1}\right]=

\displaystyle\mathrm{E}\left[\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}^{{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}+\eta_{t}^{-1}\boldsymbol{I}_{d}\right)\sum_{s=1}^{t-1}y_{s}(1)\frac{\mathbf{1}[Z_{s}=1]}{p_{s}}\cdot\boldsymbol{x}_{s}\Big|\mathcal{F}_{t-1}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}^{{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}+\eta_{t}^{-1}\boldsymbol{I}_{d}\right)\sum_{s=1}^{t-1}y_{s}(1)\cdot\boldsymbol{x}_{s}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}_{t}(1)\kern 5.0pt.

∎

We now state the well-known Hardy’s inequality, which will be used to bound the fourth moment of the online residuals in Lemma
B.7
.

Proposition B.6

(Hardy’s inequality)
.

If
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}
is a sequence of non-negative real numbers, then for every real number
p>1
one has

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{n}\left(\frac{1}{k}\sum_{r=1}^{k}a_{r}\right)^{p}\leq\left(\frac{p}{p-1}\right)^{p}\sum_{k=1}^{n}a_{k}^{p}\kern 5.0pt.

Lemma B.7
.

Under Assumption
1
-
2
, for
k\in\{0,1\}
, there holds:

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}\eta_{t}\left(y_{t}(k)-\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}_{t}(k)\rangle\right)^{4}\leq

\displaystyle c_{1}^{4}(1+2^{-1/2}(\gamma_{0}\vee 4c_{2})^{1/2})^{4}R_{T}^{2}T^{1/2}\kern 5.0pt.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we only prove the result for
k=1
.
By Hölder’s inequality, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}\eta_{t}\left(y_{t}(1)-\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}_{t}(1)\rangle\right)^{4}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}\eta_{t}\left[y_{t}^{4}(1)-4y_{t}^{3}(1)\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}_{t}(1)\rangle+6y_{t}^{2}(1)\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}_{t}(1)\rangle^{2}-4y_{t}(1)\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}_{t}(1)\rangle^{3}+\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}_{t}(1)\rangle^{4}\right]^{4}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}\eta_{t}y_{t}^{4}(1)+4\left(\sum_{t=1}^{T}\eta_{t}y_{t}^{4}(1)\right)^{3/4}\left(\sum_{t=1}^{T}\eta_{t}\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}_{t}(1)\rangle^{4}\right)^{1/4}+6\left(\sum_{t=1}^{T}\eta_{t}y_{t}^{4}(1)\right)^{1/2}\left(\sum_{t=1}^{T}\eta_{t}\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}_{t}(1)\rangle^{4}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle+4\left(\sum_{t=1}^{T}\eta_{t}y_{t}^{4}(1)\right)^{1/4}\left(\sum_{t=1}^{T}\eta_{t}\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}_{t}(1)\rangle^{4}\right)^{3/4}+\left(\sum_{t=1}^{T}\eta_{t}\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}_{t}(1)\rangle^{4}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left[\left(\sum_{t=1}^{T}\eta_{t}y_{t}^{4}(1)\right)^{1/4}+\left(\sum_{t=1}^{T}\eta_{t}\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}_{t}(1)\rangle^{4}\right)^{1/4}\right]^{4}\quad(\text{H{\"{o}}lder's inequality})\kern 5.0pt.

(2)

Denote
\boldsymbol{Y}_{t-1}=(y_{1}(1),\ldots,y_{t-1}(1))^{{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}}
.
By Lemma
B.3
, for any
t\in[T]
, we have

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}^{*}(1)\|_{2}^{2}=

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Y}_{t-1}^{{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}^{{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}+\eta_{t}^{-1}\boldsymbol{I}_{d}\right)^{-2}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}^{{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}}\boldsymbol{Y}_{t-1}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{Y}_{t-1}\right\|_{2}^{2}\left\|\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}^{{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}+\eta_{t}^{-1}\boldsymbol{I}_{d}\right)^{-2}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}^{{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\gamma_{0}}{4}\vee c_{2}\right)(t-1)^{-1}\left(\sum_{s=1}^{t-1}y_{s}^{2}(1)\right)\quad(\text{Lemma \ref{lem4.1}})\kern 5.0pt.

(3)

By (
B.2
), Hardy’s inequality (Proposition
B.6
) and Assumption
1
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}\eta_{t}\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}_{t}(1)\rangle^{4}\leq

\displaystyle T^{-1/2}\sum_{t=1}^{T}R_{t}^{-2}\left\|\boldsymbol{x}_{t}\right\|_{2}^{4}\left\|\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}^{*}(1)\right\|_{2}^{4}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle T^{-1/2}\sum_{t=1}^{T}R_{t}^{2}\left\|\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}^{*}(1)\right\|_{2}^{4}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\gamma_{0}}{4}\vee c_{2}\right)^{2}R_{T}^{2}T^{-1/2}\sum_{t=2}^{T}\left(\frac{1}{t-1}\sum_{s=1}^{t-1}y_{s}^{2}(1)\right)^{2}\quad(\text{by \eqref{lem4.5_eq} and $R_{t}\leq R_{T}$})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\gamma_{0}}{4}\vee c_{2}\right)^{2}R_{T}^{2}T^{-1/2}\left(\frac{2}{2-1}\right)^{2}\sum_{t=1}^{T-1}y_{s}^{4}(1)\quad(\text{Hardy's inequality})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}c_{1}^{4}\left(\gamma_{0}\vee 4c_{2}\right)^{2}R_{T}^{2}T^{1/2}\quad(\text{Assumption \ref{assumption:moments}})\kern 5.0pt.

(4)

By Assumption
1
, it is easy to see that

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}\eta_{t}y_{t}^{4}(1)\leq T^{-1/2}\cdot c_{1}^{4}T=c_{1}^{4}T^{1/2}\kern 5.0pt.

(5)

Hence by (
B.2
), (
B.2
) and (
5
), we can prove that

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}\eta_{t}\left(y_{t}(1)-\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}_{t}(1)\rangle\right)^{4}\leq

\displaystyle\left(c_{1}T^{1/8}+2^{-1/2}c_{1}(\gamma_{0}\vee 4c_{2})^{1/2}R_{T}^{1/2}T^{1/8}\right)^{4}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(1+2^{-1/2}(\gamma_{0}\vee 4c_{2})^{1/2}R_{T}^{1/2}\right)^{4}c_{1}^{4}T^{1/2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(R_{T}^{-1/2}+2^{-1/2}(\gamma_{0}\vee 4c_{2})^{1/2}\right)^{4}c_{1}^{4}R_{T}^{2}T^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle c_{1}^{4}\left(1+2^{-1/2}(\gamma_{0}\vee 4c_{2})^{1/2}\right)^{4}R_{T}^{2}T^{1/2}\kern 5.0pt.

∎

The following corollary is a direct consequence of Lemma
B.7
.

Corollary B.8
.

Under Assumption
1
-
2
, for
k\in\{0,1\}
, there holds:

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(y_{t}(k)-\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}_{t}(k)\rangle\right)^{4}\leq

\displaystyle c_{1}^{4}\left(1+2^{-1/2}(\gamma_{0}\vee 4c_{2})^{1/2}\right)^{4}R_{T}^{4}T\kern 5.0pt.

Proof.

For any
t\in[T]
, we have
\eta_{t}^{-1}\leq\eta_{T}^{-1}=T^{1/2}R_{T}^{2}
.
Hence by Lemma
B.7
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(y_{t}(k)-\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}_{t}(k)\rangle\right)^{4}\leq

\displaystyle\eta_{T}^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\eta_{t}\left(y_{t}(k)-\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}_{t}(k)\rangle\right)^{4}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle c_{1}^{4}\left(1+2^{-1/2}(\gamma_{0}\vee 4c_{2})^{1/2}\right)^{4}R_{T}^{4}T\kern 5.0pt.

∎

Lemmas
B.9
and
B.10
provide upper bounds for deterministic
p
-series summations, which play a crucial role in establishing Lemma
B.15
.

Lemma B.9
.

For any
k>1
and fixed
t,r\in[T]
, we have
\sum_{s=t+1}^{T}((s-1)\vee\eta_{r}^{-1})^{-k}\leq\xi_{k}(t\vee\eta_{r}^{-1})^{-(k-1)}
, where
\xi_{k}\triangleq\frac{2^{k-1}}{k-1}+1>0
is a constant.

Proof.

For
t\geq\lceil\eta_{r}^{-1}\rceil+1\geq T^{1/2}+1\geq 2
, we have
(t-1)\geq t/2
. Hence we have

\displaystyle\sum_{s=t+1}^{T}((s-1)\vee\eta_{r}^{-1})^{-k}\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{s=t}^{T-1}s^{-k}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{t-1}^{T-2}s^{-k}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{k-1}\left[\left(t-1\right)^{-(k-1)}-\left(T-2\right)^{-(k-1)}\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{(t-1)^{-(k-1)}}{k-1}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{2^{k-1}t^{-(k-1)}}{k-1}\kern 5.0pt.

For
t<\lceil\eta_{r}^{-1}\rceil+1
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{s=t+1}^{T}((s-1)\vee\eta_{r}^{-1})^{-k}\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{s=\lceil\eta_{r}^{-1}\rceil+1}^{T}((s-1)\vee\eta_{r}^{-1})^{-k}+\sum_{s=2}^{\lceil\eta_{r}^{-1}\rceil}\eta_{r}^{k}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{2^{k-1}\eta_{r}^{k-1}}{k-1}+\eta_{r}^{k-1}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left(\frac{2^{k-1}}{k-1}+1\right)\eta_{r}^{k-1}\kern 5.0pt.

Hence for any fixed
t,r\in[T]
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{s=t+1}^{T}((s-1)\vee\eta_{r}^{-1})^{-k}\leq\xi_{k}(t\vee\eta_{r}^{-1})^{-(k-1)}\kern 5.0pt.

∎

Lemma B.10
.

For any
k,\nu\geq 0
such that
0<2k-\nu<2
, when
T
is large enough, there holds:

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}R_{t}^{\nu}((t-1)\vee\eta_{t}^{-1})^{-k}\leq\frac{T^{1-k+\nu/4}}{1-(k-\nu/2)}\kern 5.0pt.

Proof.

For any
t\in[T]
, a natural upper bound for
R_{t}^{\nu}((t-1)\vee\eta_{t}^{-1})^{-k}
is
R_{t}^{\nu}((t-1)\vee\eta_{t}^{-1})^{-k}\leq R_{t}^{\nu}\eta_{t}^{k}=T^{-k/2}R_{t}^{-(2k-\nu)}\leq T^{-k/2}
.
We then derive another upper bound for
R_{t}^{\nu}((t-1)\vee\eta_{t}^{-1})^{-k}
:

(1)

If
(t-1)\geq T^{1/2}R_{t}^{2}
, i.e.,
1\leq R_{t}\leq((t-1)T^{-1/2})^{1/2}
, then we have

\displaystyle R_{t}^{\nu}((t-1)\vee T^{1/2}R_{t}^{2})^{-k}=

\displaystyle R_{t}^{\nu}(t-1)^{-k}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle((t-1)T^{-1/2})^{\nu/2}(t-1)^{-k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(t-1)^{-(k-\nu/2)}T^{-\nu/4}\kern 5.0pt.

(2)

If
(t-1)\leq T^{1/2}R_{t}^{2}
, i.e.,
R_{t}\geq((t-1)T^{-1/2})^{1/2}
, then we have

\displaystyle R_{t}^{\nu}((t-1)\vee T^{1/2}R_{t}^{2})^{-k}=

\displaystyle R_{t}^{\nu}(T^{1/2}R_{t}^{2})^{-k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle T^{-k/2}R_{t}^{-(2k-\nu)}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle T^{-k/2}((t-1)T^{-1/2})^{-(2k-\nu)/2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(t-1)^{-(k-\nu/2)}T^{-\nu/4}\kern 5.0pt.

Since
0<1-(k-\nu/2)<1
by assumption, when
T
is large enough, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\left(\lfloor T^{1/2}\rfloor-1\right)^{1-(k-\nu/2)}}{1-(k-\nu/2)}=\frac{1}{1-(k-\nu/2)}\left(\frac{\lfloor T^{1/2}\rfloor-1}{T^{1/2}}\right)^{1-(k-\nu/2)}\cdot T^{1/2\cdot(1-(k-\nu/2))}\geq T^{1/2\cdot(1-(k-\nu/2))}\kern 5.0pt.

This implies that

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}R_{t}^{\nu}((t-1)\vee\eta_{t}^{-1})^{-k}\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}T^{-k/2}\land(t-1)^{-(k-\nu/2)}T^{-\nu/4}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor T^{1/2}\rfloor}T^{-k/2}+\sum_{\lfloor T^{1/2}\rfloor+1}^{T}(t-1)^{-(k-\nu/2)}T^{-\nu/4}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle T^{-k/2}\cdot\lfloor T^{1/2}\rfloor+T^{-\nu/4}\int_{\lfloor T^{1/2}\rfloor-1}^{T-2}t^{-(k-\nu/2)}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle T^{-k/2}\cdot\lfloor T^{1/2}\rfloor+\frac{T^{-\nu/4}}{1-(k-\nu/2)}\left[(T-2)^{1-(k-\nu/2)}-\left(\lfloor T^{1/2}\rfloor-1\right)^{1-(k-\nu/2)}\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle T^{-(k-1)/2}-\frac{T^{-\nu/4}\left(\lfloor T^{1/2}\rfloor-1\right)^{1-(k-\nu/2)}}{1-(k-\nu/2)}+\frac{T^{-\nu/4+1-(k-\nu/2)}}{1-(k-\nu/2)}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle T^{-(k-1)/2}-T^{-\nu/4}\cdot T^{1/2\cdot(1-(k-\nu/2))}+\frac{T^{-\nu/4+1-(k-\nu/2)}}{1-(k-\nu/2)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{T^{1-k+\nu/4}}{1-(k-\nu/2)}\kern 5.0pt.

∎

The following Lemma
B.11
has similar proof as in Lemma
4.9
, Lemma
4.10
and Proposition
4.11
.
However, we present an alternative proof that does not rely on the fourth-moment condition in Assumption
1
.
Throughout the proofs of Lemma
B.11
, Corollary
B.12
and Lemma
B.13
, we instead impose only the second-moment assumption (1*):

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}y_{t}^{2}(k)\leq c_{1}^{2}T\kern 5.0pt.

This assumption is weaker than the Assumption
1
, which plays a crucial role in establishing Lemma
B.13
.

Lemma B.11
.

Under Assumption
1
-
3
, for
k\in\{0,1\}
, there holds
A_{T}^{*}(k)\leq 2c_{1}^{2}T
for
T
large enough.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we only prove the result for
k=1
.
By the second moment assumption (1*), we can easily see that
\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(y_{t}(1)-\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}(1)\rangle\right)^{2}\leq\sum_{t=1}^{T}y_{t}^{2}(1)\leq c_{1}^{2}T
.
By Corollary
B.4
, Assumption
2
, and similar proofs as in Lemma
4.9
and Lemma
4.10
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(y_{t}(1)-\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}^{*}(1)\rangle\right)^{2}-\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(y_{t}(1)-\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}(1)\rangle\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\eta_{T+1}^{-1}\|\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}(1)\|_{2}^{2}+\sum_{t=1}^{T}(\tilde{L}_{t+1}^{(1)}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}_{t}(1))-\tilde{L}_{t+1}^{(1)}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}_{t+1}(1)))\quad(\text{similar proof as in Lemma \ref{lemma:standard-oco-for-pred-regret}})

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\eta_{T+1}^{-1}\|\boldsymbol{Y}_{T}(1)\|_{2}^{2}\left\|\boldsymbol{X}_{T}\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{T}^{{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}}\boldsymbol{X}_{T}\right)^{-2}\boldsymbol{X}_{T}^{{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}}\right\|_{2}+\sum_{t=1}^{T}\Pi_{t,t}\left(y_{t}(1)-\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}^{*}(1)\rangle\right)^{2}\quad(\text{similar proof as in Lemma \ref{lemma:ptpredloss-diff}})

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\eta_{T}^{-1}+R_{t}^{2}\eta_{t}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(y_{t}(1)-\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}^{*}(1)\rangle\right)^{2}\quad(\text{Corollary \ref{coro4.1} and Assumption \ref{assumption:covariate-regularity}})\kern 5.0pt.

(6)

Note that
R_{t}^{2}\eta_{t}=T^{-1/2}=o(1)
and
\eta_{T}^{-1}=T^{1/2}R_{T}^{2}=o(T)
by Assumption
3
.
Hence (
B.2
) implies that
A_{T}^{*}(1)=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(y_{t}(1)-\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}^{*}(1)\rangle\right)^{2}\leq 2\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(y_{t}(1)-\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}(1)\rangle\right)^{2}\leq 2c_{1}^{2}T
when
T
is large enough.
∎

The following corollary is a direct result of Lemma
B.11
.

Corollary B.12
.

Under Assumption
1
-
3
, for
k\in\{0,1\}
, there holds:
\sum_{t=1}^{T}\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}_{t}(1)\rangle^{2}\leq(2^{1/2}+1)^{2}c_{1}^{2}T
for
T
large enough.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we only prove the result for
k=1
.
By Lemma
B.11
and Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, for large enough
T
, we have

\displaystyle 2c_{1}^{2}T\geq

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(y_{t}(1)-\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}_{t}(1)\rangle\right)^{2}~~~~(\text{Lemma \ref{coro4.3}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}y_{t}^{2}-2\sum_{t=1}^{T}y_{t}(1)\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}_{t}(1)\rangle+\sum_{t=1}^{T}\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}_{t}(1)\rangle^{2}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}y_{t}^{2}-2\left(\sum_{t=1}^{T}y_{t}^{2}\right)^{1/2}\left(\sum_{t=1}^{T}\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}_{t}(1)\rangle^{2}\right)^{1/2}+\sum_{t=1}^{T}\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}_{t}(1)\rangle^{2}~~~~(\text{Cauchy-Schwarz inequality})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[\left(\sum_{t=1}^{T}y_{t}^{2}\right)^{1/2}-\left(\sum_{t=1}^{T}\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}_{t}(1)\rangle^{2}\right)^{1/2}\right]^{2}\kern 5.0pt.

By the second moment assumption (1*), we have
\left(\sum_{t=1}^{T}y_{t}^{2}\right)^{1/2}\leq c_{1}T^{1/2}
. Hence it is easy to see that
\left(\sum_{t=1}^{T}\langle x_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}^{*}(1)\rangle^{2}\right)^{1/2}\leq(2^{1/2}+1)c_{1}T^{1/2}
, which indicates that
\sum_{t=1}^{T}\langle x_{t},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{t}^{*}(1)\rangle^{2}\leq(2^{1/2}+1)^{2}c_{1}^{2}T
for
T
large enough.
∎

Based on Corollary
B.12
, we can establish the following lemma.

Lemma B.13
.

Suppose
T
is large enough. Under Assumption
1
-
3
, for any
2\leq t\leq T
, let the entries in matrix
\breve{\boldsymbol{Q}}^{(t)}=(\breve{Q}_{i,j}^{(t)})\in\mathbb{R}_{(t-1)\times(t-1)}
be
\breve{Q}_{i,j}^{(t)}=\sum_{s=i\vee j+1}^{t}\Pi_{s,i}\Pi_{s,j}
for any
1\leq i,j\leq t-1
. Then
\breve{\boldsymbol{Q}}^{(t)}
is a semi-definite positive matrix and there holds:
\|\breve{\boldsymbol{Q}}^{(t)}\|_{2}\leq(2^{1/2}+1)^{2}
.

Proof.

By direct calculation, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{s=1}^{T}\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{s},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{s}^{*}(1)\rangle^{2}\geq

\displaystyle\sum_{s=1}^{t}\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{s},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{s}^{*}(1)\rangle^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{s=1}^{t}\left(\sum_{r=1}^{s-1}\Pi_{s,r}y_{r}(1)\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{s=1}^{t}\sum_{1\leq r_{1},r_{2}\leq s-1}\Pi_{s,r_{1}}\Pi_{s,r_{2}}y_{r_{1}}(1)y_{r_{2}}(1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq r_{1},r_{2}\leq t-1}\left(\sum_{s=r_{1}\vee r_{2}+1}^{t}\Pi_{s,r_{1}}\Pi_{s,r_{2}}\right)y_{r_{1}}(1)y_{r_{2}}(1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{r_{1}=1}^{t-1}\sum_{r_{2}=1}^{t-1}\breve{Q}_{r_{1},r_{2}}^{(t)}y_{r_{1}}(1)y_{r_{2}}(1)\kern 5.0pt.

Note that the proof in Lemma
B.11
and Corollary
B.12
only rely on the second moment assumption (1*):
\sum_{t=1}^{T}y_{t}^{2}(1)\leq c_{1}^{2}T
(we do not use the fourth moment assumption).
Hence for
T
large enough, we have

(1)

\sum_{s_{1}=1}^{t-1}\sum_{s_{2}=1}^{t-1}\breve{Q}_{s_{1},s_{2}}^{(t)}y_{s_{1}}(1)y_{s_{2}}(1)\geq 0
, which implies that
\breve{\boldsymbol{Q}}^{(t)}
is semi-definite positive.

(2)

\sum_{s_{1}=1}^{t-1}\sum_{s_{2}=1}^{t-1}\breve{Q}_{s_{1},s_{2}}^{(t)}y_{s_{1}}(1)y_{s_{2}}(1)\leq(2^{1/2}+1)^{2}c_{1}^{2}T
, which implies that
\|\breve{\boldsymbol{Q}}^{(t)}\|_{2}\leq(2^{1/2}+1)^{2}
.

∎

Based on Lemma
B.13
, we can derive the following corollary.

Corollary B.14
.

Suppose
T
is large enough. Under Assumption
1
-
3
, for a fixed
t\in[T]
, and any
1\leq s\leq t
, let the entries in matrix
\boldsymbol{Q}^{(s,t)}=(Q_{i,j}^{(s,t)})\in\mathbb{R}_{(t-s)\times(t-s)}
be
Q^{(s,t)}_{i,j}=(\sum_{r=i\vee j+s}^{t}\Pi_{r,i+s-1}\Pi_{r,j+s-1})^{2}\left(\frac{R_{t}}{R_{i+s-1}}\cdot\frac{R_{t}}{R_{j+s-1}}\right)^{1/2}
for any
1\leq i,j\leq t-s
. Then
\boldsymbol{Q}^{(s,t)}
is a semi-definite positive matrix and there holds:
\|\boldsymbol{Q}^{(s,t)}\|_{2}\leq 2(2^{1/2}+1)^{2}(\gamma_{0}\vee c_{2}\vee 1)^{2}R_{t}^{4}(s\vee\eta_{t}^{-1})^{-1}
.

Proof.

For any
1\leq s\leq t\leq T
, let the entries in matrix
\tilde{\boldsymbol{Q}}^{(s,t)}=(\tilde{Q}_{i,j}^{(s,t)})\in\mathbb{R}_{(t-s)\times(t-s)}
be
\tilde{Q}^{(s,t)}_{i,j}=(\sum_{r=i\vee j+s}^{t}\Pi_{r,i+s-1}\Pi_{r,j+s-1})
for any
1\leq i,j\leq t-s
and let the entries in matrix
\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}^{(s,t)}=(\bar{Q}_{i,j}^{(s,t)})\in\mathbb{R}_{(t-s)\times(t-s)}
be
\bar{Q}^{(s,t)}_{i,j}=(\sum_{r=i\vee j+s}^{t}\Pi_{r,i+s-1}\Pi_{r,j+s-1})\left(\frac{R_{t}}{R_{i+s-1}}\cdot\frac{R_{t}}{R_{j+s-1}}\right)^{1/2}
for any
1\leq i,j\leq t-s
. Since
\tilde{\boldsymbol{Q}}^{(s,t)}
is a principal submatrix of semi-definite positive matrix
\breve{\boldsymbol{Q}}^{(t)}
,
\tilde{\boldsymbol{Q}}^{(s,t)}
is semi-definite positive. By the definition of
\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}^{(s,t)}
, we have

\displaystyle\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}^{(s,t)}=\operatorname{diag}\left\{\left(\frac{R_{t}}{R_{s}}\right)^{1/2},\ldots,\left(\frac{R_{t}}{R_{t-1}}\right)^{1/2}\right\}\tilde{\boldsymbol{Q}}^{(s,t)}\operatorname{diag}\left\{\left(\frac{R_{t}}{R_{t_{1}}}\right)^{1/2},\ldots,\left(\frac{R_{t}}{R_{t-1}}\right)^{1/2}\right\}\kern 5.0pt.

Hence
\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}^{(s,t)}
is also semi-definite positive. This implies that
\boldsymbol{Q}^{(s,t)}=\tilde{\boldsymbol{Q}}^{(s,t)}\circ\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}^{(s,t)}
is semi-definite positive. Now we prove the second part. For any
1\leq k\leq t-1
, we aim to bound the
k
-th diagonal element of
\bar{\boldsymbol{Q}}^{(1,t)}
.
We derive the upper in two different cases. If
1\leq k\leq\eta_{t}^{-1}
, we have

\displaystyle\bar{Q}^{(1,t)}_{k,k}=

\displaystyle\frac{R_{t}}{R_{k}}\cdot\sum_{s=k+1}^{t}\Pi_{s,k}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(\gamma_{0}\vee c_{2}\vee 1)^{2}\frac{R_{t}}{R_{k}}\cdot\sum_{s=k+1}^{t}R_{s}^{2}R_{k}^{2}((s-1)\vee\eta_{s}^{-1})^{-2}~~~~(\text{Corollary \ref{coro4.1}})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(\gamma_{0}\vee c_{2}\vee 1)^{2}R_{t}R_{k}\sum_{s=k+1}^{\lfloor\eta_{t}^{-1}\rfloor+2}R_{s}^{2}((s-1)\vee\eta_{s}^{-1})^{-2}+(\gamma_{0}\vee c_{2}\vee 1)^{2}R_{t}\sum_{s=\lfloor\eta_{t}^{-1}\rfloor+3}^{t}R_{s}^{3}((s-1)\vee\eta_{s}^{-1})^{-2}

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle S_{1}+S_{2}\kern 5.0pt.

(7)

By calculation, it is easy to see that function
x^{-1/2}(2+\log x)
has maximum value 2 on
[1,\infty)
.
Hence we have

\displaystyle S_{1}\leq

\displaystyle(\gamma_{0}\vee c_{2}\vee 1)^{2}T^{-1/2}R_{t}R_{k}\sum_{s=k+1}^{\lfloor\eta_{t}^{-1}\rfloor+2}\eta_{s}^{-1}((s-1)\vee\eta_{s}^{-1})^{-2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(\gamma_{0}\vee c_{2}\vee 1)^{2}R_{t}^{2}\cdot\frac{R_{k}}{R_{t}}T^{-1/2}\sum_{s=k+1}^{\lfloor\eta_{t}^{-1}\rfloor+2}((s-1)\vee\eta_{s}^{-1})^{-1}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(\gamma_{0}\vee c_{2}\vee 1)^{2}R_{t}^{2}\cdot\frac{R_{k}}{R_{t}}T^{-1/2}\left(\sum_{s=1}^{\lfloor\eta_{k+1}^{-1}\rfloor+2}\eta_{k+1}+\sum_{s=\lfloor\eta_{k+1}^{-1}\rfloor+3}^{\lfloor\eta_{t}^{-1}\rfloor+2}(s-1)^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(\gamma_{0}\vee c_{2}\vee 1)^{2}R_{t}^{2}\cdot\frac{R_{k}}{R_{t}}T^{-1/2}\left(\sum_{s=1}^{\lfloor\eta_{k+1}^{-1}\rfloor+2}\eta_{k+1}+\int_{\lfloor\eta_{k+1}^{-1}\rfloor+1}^{\lfloor\eta_{t}^{-1}\rfloor}s^{-1}\mathrm{d}s\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(\gamma_{0}\vee c_{2}\vee 1)^{2}R_{t}^{2}\cdot\frac{R_{k}}{R_{t}}T^{-1/2}\left(\eta_{k+1}\left(\lfloor\eta_{k+1}^{-1}\rfloor+2\right)+\log(\lfloor\eta_{t}^{-1}\rfloor)-\log(\lfloor\eta_{k+1}^{-1}\rfloor+1)\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(\gamma_{0}\vee c_{2}\vee 1)^{2}R_{t}^{2}\cdot\left(\frac{\eta_{t}}{\eta_{k}}\right)^{1/2}T^{-1/2}\left(2+\log(\lfloor\eta_{t}^{-1}\rfloor\eta_{k})\right)~~~~(\text{since $T$ large enough and $\eta_{k+1}\leq\eta_{k}$})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\gamma_{0}\vee c_{2}\vee 1)^{2}R_{t}^{2}\cdot\left(\frac{\eta_{t}}{\eta_{k}}\right)^{1/2}T^{-1/2}\left(2+\log(\lfloor\eta_{t}^{-1}\rfloor\eta_{t})+\log\left(\frac{\eta_{k}}{\eta_{t}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(\gamma_{0}\vee c_{2}\vee 1)^{2}R_{t}^{2}\cdot\left(\frac{\eta_{t}}{\eta_{k}}\right)^{1/2}T^{-1/2}\left(2+\log\left(\frac{\eta_{k}}{\eta_{t}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2(\gamma_{0}\vee c_{2}\vee 1)^{2}R_{t}^{2}T^{-1/2}~~~~(\text{since $\eta_{k}/\eta_{t}\geq 1$})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2(\gamma_{0}\vee c_{2}\vee 1)^{2}R_{t}^{4}\eta_{t}\kern 5.0pt.

(8)

By direct calculation, we have

\displaystyle S_{2}\leq

\displaystyle(\gamma_{0}\vee c_{2}\vee 1)^{2}R_{t}\sum_{s=\lfloor\eta_{t}^{-1}\rfloor+3}^{t}((s-1)R_{s}^{-2}\vee T^{1/2})^{-3/2}((s-1)\vee\eta_{s}^{-1})^{-1/2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(\gamma_{0}\vee c_{2}\vee 1)^{2}R_{t}\sum_{s=\lfloor\eta_{t}^{-1}\rfloor+3}^{t}((s-1)R_{t}^{-2}\vee T^{1/2})^{-3/2}\cdot(s-1)^{-1/2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(\gamma_{0}\vee c_{2}\vee 1)^{2}R_{t}^{4}\eta_{t}^{1/2}\sum_{s=\lfloor\eta_{t}^{-1}\rfloor+3}^{t}((s-1)\vee\eta_{t}^{-1})^{-3/2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(\gamma_{0}\vee c_{2}\vee 1)^{2}R_{t}^{4}\eta_{t}^{1/2}\sum_{s=\lfloor\eta_{t}^{-1}\rfloor+3}^{t}(s-1)^{-3/2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(\gamma_{0}\vee c_{2}\vee 1)^{2}R_{t}^{4}\eta_{t}^{1/2}\int_{\lfloor\eta_{t}^{-1}\rfloor+1}^{t-2}(s-1)^{-3/2}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2(\gamma_{0}\vee c_{2}\vee 1)^{2}R_{t}^{4}\eta_{t}^{1/2}(\lfloor\eta_{t}^{-1}\rfloor+1)^{-1/2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2(\gamma_{0}\vee c_{2}\vee 1)^{2}R_{t}^{4}\eta_{t}\kern 5.0pt.

(9)

Hence by (
B.2
), (
B.2
) and (
B.2
), for any
1\leq k\leq\lceil\eta_{t}^{-1}\rceil
, we can derive the following upper bound:

\displaystyle\bar{Q}^{(1,t)}_{k,k}\leq S_{1}+S_{2}\leq 4(\gamma_{0}\vee c_{2}\vee 1)^{2}R_{t}^{4}\eta_{t}\kern 5.0pt.

(10)

For any
\eta_{t}^{-1}\leq k\leq t
, by Corollary
B.4
we have

\displaystyle\bar{Q}^{(1,t)}_{k,k}=

\displaystyle\frac{R_{t}}{R_{k}}\cdot\sum_{s=k+1}^{t}\Pi_{s,k}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(\gamma_{0}\vee c_{2}\vee 1)^{2}\frac{R_{t}}{R_{k}}\cdot\sum_{s=k+1}^{t}R_{s}^{2}R_{k}^{2}((s-1)\vee\eta_{s}^{-1})^{-2}~~~~(\text{Corollary \ref{coro4.1}})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(\gamma_{0}\vee c_{2}\vee 1)^{2}\frac{R_{t}}{R_{k}}\cdot R_{t}^{2}R_{k}^{2}\cdot\sum_{s=k+1}^{t}(s-1)^{-2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(\gamma_{0}\vee c_{2}\vee 1)^{2}R_{t}^{4}\int_{k-1}^{t-2}s^{-2}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2(\gamma_{0}\vee c_{2}\vee 1)^{2}R_{t}^{4}k^{-1}\quad(\text{since $T$ is large enough})\kern 5.0pt.

(11)

By (
10
) and (
B.2
), we have
\bar{Q}^{(1,t)}_{k,k}\leq 4(\gamma_{0}\vee c_{2}\vee 1)^{2}R_{t}^{4}(k\vee\eta_{t}^{-1})^{-1}
.
Since
\tilde{\boldsymbol{Q}}^{(s,t)}
is a principal submatrix of
\breve{\boldsymbol{Q}}^{(t)}
, we have
